## User

**Tool Result:** `toolu_vrtx_01QX12jDk8Vhtpe6vm8deVZw`

```
1) TL;DR
A 1px pixel-drawn separator between segments is very feasible with your current softbuffer/fontdue setup and is a good fit for the new structured status bar. Treat it as a rendering/layout detail (not a segment), use char-based spacing but draw the separator as a vertical line in pixel space, and expose color/spacing via the theme. Effort: S (once the segment layout is in place).

---

2) Recommended approach (simple path)

**High-level idea:**
Keep the logical layout in “character cells” as in `StatusBar::layout`, but render separators as 1px vertical lines in the status bar background instead of a `│` glyph.

### a. Integrate with the current rendering

1. **Extend layout to expose separator regions (char units):**

   In `StatusBar::layout` (design doc), you already have:

   ```rust
   let separator_width = self.separator.chars().count();
   ```

   Keep that, but interpret it as *logical spacing* between segments, not text to be rendered.

   For left segments:

   ```rust
   // Pseudocode modification
   let mut separators_left: Vec<usize> = Vec::new();
   for (i, seg) in self.left.iter().enumerate() {
       if seg.content.is_empty() { continue; }

       if i > 0 {
           // Reserve separator_width chars of space
           left_x += separator_width;
           // Logical separator center in char units
           let sep_center = left_x - separator_width / 2;
           separators_left.push(sep_center);
       }

       let width = seg.content.char_width();
       left_segments.push(RenderedSegment { id: seg.id, x: left_x, width, ... });
       left_x += width;
   }
   ```

   Do analogous logic for `right` (and later `center`).

   Extend `StatusBarLayout`:

   ```rust
   struct StatusBarLayout {
       left: Vec<RenderedSegment>,
       center: Vec<RenderedSegment>,
       right: Vec<RenderedSegment>,
       separator_positions: Vec<usize>, // char positions (logical centers)
   }
   ```

2. **Convert separator positions to pixels and draw lines:**

   In `Renderer::render_impl`, when you render the status bar:

   - You already compute:

     ```rust
     let line_height = self.line_metrics.new_line_size.ceil() as usize;
     let status_height = line_height;
     let status_y = (height as usize).saturating_sub(status_height);
     ```

   - After filling the status bar background, and before drawing segment text, do:

     ```rust
     let sep_color = model.theme.status_bar.separator_color.to_argb_u32();
     let char_width = self.char_width; // f32
     let margin_px = (0.5 * char_width).round() as usize; // example

     for sep_char_x in layout.separator_positions.iter() {
         let x_px = ((*sep_char_x as f32) * char_width).round() as usize;

         // Optional vertical insets for aesthetics
         let y_start = status_y + 2;
         let y_end   = (height as usize).saturating_sub(2);

         // Draw 1px vertical line
         for py in y_start..y_end {
             if x_px < width as usize {
                 buffer[py * width as usize + x_px] = sep_color;
             }
         }
     }
     ```

   - You can ignore `margin_px` at first; logically the separator spacing (in chars) already creates margins. If you want fancier shading, you can later tweak pixels to the left/right of the line.

3. **Render text segments as usual:**

   - Use `layout.left[i].x` and `layout.right[i].x` (char units) as before; convert to pixels:

     ```rust
     let x_px = (layout_seg.x as f32 * char_width).round() as usize;
     ```

   - Call `draw_text` with these `x_px` values; `draw_text` + fontdue keep handling glyph rasterization, cache, etc. No changes required inside fontdue integration.

4. **No change to glyph rasterization or CPU rendering path:**

   - This stays a pure “write ARGB to the softbuffer” operation, same as current gutter border (see lines 458–466 of `main.rs`), so it fits naturally:

     ```rust
     // Gutter border example
     for py in 0..status_y_top {
         if border_x < width as usize {
             buffer[py * width as usize + border_x] = gutter_border_color;
         }
     }
     ```

   - The separator lines are drawn almost identically but restricted to the status bar’s y-range.

---

3) Should the separator be a segment type or a rendering detail?

**Recommendation:** treat separators as a **rendering/layout detail**, not as `StatusSegment` entries.

- They:
  - Have no semantic meaning.
  - Aren’t interactive.
  - Are purely visual grouping elements between real segments.

**Implementation implications:**

- Keep `StatusBar.left/right/center` as lists of *content* segments only.
- `StatusBar::layout` is responsible for:
  - Deciding where separators go (between non-empty neighbors).
  - Returning those positions in `StatusBarLayout`.
- The renderer:
  - Always draws separators between visible segments.
  - Can easily turn them off or style them globally via theme.

You can keep `StatusBar.separator` as something like `"   "` only to represent *spacing in chars* (i.e., `separator_width`), not literal text to draw.

---

4) Trade-offs vs character-based separators

**Pros of pixel-drawn separators (lines):**

- **Visual quality / modern look:**
  - Can be thinner than a full character cell.
  - Can span the full status bar height instead of sitting on baseline.
  - Color decoupled from text foreground; matches gut­ter border style.

- **Space efficiency:**
  - You’re no longer forced to spend a full monospaced char on the bar; the line can sit anywhere inside a multi-char “gap.”
  - Easy to add subtle 1–2px padding around the line without burning visible characters.

- **Consistency with non-text UI:**
  - Your renderer already draws rectangles and borders; this aligns with that approach and makes future UI bits (icons, highlights) easier to mix in.

**Cons / costs:**

- **Slightly more layout complexity:**
  - You must track logical separator regions and convert to pixel x-coordinates.
  - But you’re already computing layout; adding a list of separator positions is trivial.

- **Harder to inspect with “show invisibles”:**
  - A `|` char is in the text model; you can print or log the exact status bar string.
  - Separators as drawing detail don’t show up in any textual representation (though your new structured status bar design already abandons “pure string” anyway).

- **Theming customization via text is gone:**
  - You can’t just change the separator glyph; instead you change spacing + separator color.
  - For most users this is an improvement (more styling control), but it’s a different customization surface.

Given you’re moving to structured segments with explicit layout, the **benefits clearly outweigh the minor complexity**.

---

5) How should margins be defined (pixels vs char_width)?

**Recommendation:** define margins logically in **character units**, but implement in **pixels**.

- Your layout logic is already char-based (`x: usize` “character position”, `content.char_width()`).
- Keep this mental model and avoid mixing char/pixel units in the higher-level API.

Concretely:

1. Let `separator_width_chars: usize` (e.g., from `self.separator.chars().count()`), default 2 or 3.
2. Use that to update `left_x` / `right_x` as in the design doc.
3. Compute the separator’s center in char units:

   ```rust
   let sep_center_char = left_x - separator_width_chars / 2;
   ```

4. Convert to pixels in the renderer:

   ```rust
   let sep_x_px = (sep_center_char as f32 * char_width).round() as usize;
   ```

5. The **margins** on each side of the line are then `(separator_width_chars * char_width - 1px) / 2` in pixel space. That gives you spacing that scales correctly with font size and DPI without extra configuration.

If you later want fine-grained control (e.g., 4px fixed margins even if font changes), you can add a `separator_margin_px: u8` in the theme and offset `sep_x_px` by that. For v1, char-relative spacing is simpler and good enough.

---

6) Is this approach common in other editors?

Yes, it matches how many modern GUI editors handle status bars:

- **VS Code:**
  - Status bar items (encoding, line ending, Git branch, etc.) are separated by subtle 1px-ish vertical rules (`border-left` in CSS) and padding, not by literal `|` characters.
- **JetBrains IDEs (IntelliJ, CLion, etc.):**
  - Use segmented status bar “widgets” with borders and spacing; separators are visual borders, not text.
- **Sublime Text:**
  - Often relies more on spacing and sectioned background tints, but where separators appear they are drawn, not text.
- **Terminal editors (Vim/Neovim, terminal Emacs):**
  - Tend to use `|` or other characters because they’re constrained to text-based rendering. Your renderer is not, so you can adopt the GUI-style look.

So a pixel line separator is both **feasible with your stack** and **aligned with common GUI editor UX**.

---

7) Risks and guardrails

- **HiDPI / scaling artifacts:**
  - You’re already scaling `font_size` and `char_width` with the window scale factor; just ensure you derive separator `x_px` from the same `char_width` so lines stay aligned and crisp.
- **Overcrowding in narrow windows:**
  - Too many separators in a cramped bar can look noisy. Guard by:
    - Using small `separator_width_chars` (e.g., 2).
    - Potentially dropping low-priority segments before drawing, as your design already anticipates via `priority`.
- **Future center alignment:**
  - When you implement the center group, just apply the same separator logic group-wise (between segments in the same group). You probably don’t want separators between left/center/right boundaries, to avoid awkward central lines.

---

8) When to consider the advanced path

You might revisit the design if:

- You want **per-segment separators** (some segments grouped without separators, others with).
- You want **different separator styles based on state** (e.g., red separator near an error segment).
- You implement **hover/click** behavior on separators themselves (e.g., drag to resize segment clusters).

At that point, modeling separators as lightweight “pseudo-segments” (with id, style, no content) could make sense.

---

9) Optional advanced path (brief)

If you later need more control:

- Introduce:

  ```rust
  pub enum StatusBarItem {
      Segment(StatusSegment),
      Separator, // styled via theme
  }
  ```

- Make `StatusBar.left/center/right: Vec<StatusBarItem>`.
- Layout and render treat `Segment` vs `Separator` differently:
  - `Segment` reserves width based on content.
  - `Separator` consumes `separator_width_chars` and has its own style.

That’s a heavier but more flexible model. For now, the **simple, rendering-only separator lines** give you 90% of the benefit with minimal complexity.
```

## Assistant

### Oracle Findings

**Verdict: Yes, this is a sane and recommended approach.**

#### Key Points:

1. **Rendering Feasibility** - Works perfectly with your existing softbuffer approach. You already draw 1px vertical lines for the gutter border (lines 458-466 in main.rs). Same technique applies.

2. **Separator as Rendering Detail (not a segment type)** - Oracle recommends treating separators as a **layout/rendering concern**, not as `StatusSegment` entries:
   - No semantic meaning
   - Not interactive
   - Purely visual grouping

3. **How It Works**:
   - Keep `separator_width_chars` (e.g., 2-3) for logical spacing between segments
   - Track separator center positions in char units during layout
   - Convert to pixel `x` coordinate when rendering
   - Draw 1px vertical line at that position

4. **Margins** - Defined in char units, rendered in pixels. The margin is automatically `(separator_width_chars * char_width - 1px) / 2` on each side.

5. **Industry Standard** - VS Code, JetBrains IDEs, and Sublime all use pixel-drawn separators/borders, not `|` characters. Terminal editors use `|` only because they're text-constrained.

#### Trade-offs:
| Pros | Cons |
|------|------|
| Thinner, modern look | Slightly more layout complexity |
| Space efficient | Can't inspect as text |
| Consistent with other UI elements | Different customization surface |
| Scales with DPI properly | |

**Recommendation**: Update the design doc to use char-based spacing with a rendered 1px line, expose `separator_color` and `separator_margin_px` in theme. This is lower complexity than making separators a segment type.

Should I update the design doc with this approach?

Updated the design doc with:

1. **`separator_spacing: usize`** replaces `separator: String` - defines spacing in character units (default 2 = 1 char margin each side)
2. **`separator_positions: Vec<usize>`** in layout output - tracks where to draw the 1px lines
3. **`separator_color`** in theme - documented for the rendered line color
4. **New `render_separators()` function** - shows how to draw 1px vertical lines (matching gutter border pattern)
5. **Updated examples** - clarified that `│` represents a rendered pixel line, not a character

## User

**Tool Result:** `toolu_vrtx_018wQEhfRCdFB6XtPZe2gUgY`

```
1) **TL;DR**

- Put YAML files in a top‑level `themes/` directory and embed them with `include_str!` in `theme.rs`.
- Treat the default theme as YAML-backed, but keep the current Rust `default_dark()` as a silent fallback if YAML parsing fails.
- Add a small static registry of built‑in themes for future UI listing; keep auto‑discovery via `build.rs` as an optional “advanced path.”

Effort: **S (≤1h)** for the simple path; **M (1–3h)** if you also add build.rs auto-discovery.


---

2) **Recommended approach (simple path)**

### 2.1 Folder structure

At repo root:

```text
.
├── Cargo.toml
├── src/
│   ├── main.rs
│   └── theme.rs
└── themes/
    ├── default-dark.yaml
    ├── fleet-dark.yaml
    └── github-dark.yaml
```

- `themes/default-dark.yaml` – source of truth for the current `default_dark` colors.
- `themes/fleet-dark.yaml` – content currently in `FLEET_DARK_YAML`.
- `themes/github-dark.yaml` – content currently in `GITHUB_DARK_YAML`.

You can use `default.yaml` instead of `default-dark.yaml` if you prefer, but I’d keep the `-dark` suffix to match the others and be explicit.

### 2.2 Embed themes with `include_str!`

In `src/theme.rs`, replace the big YAML string consts with include_str!-based consts:

```rust
// At top-level in theme.rs

pub const DEFAULT_DARK_YAML: &str = include_str!("../themes/default-dark.yaml");
pub const FLEET_DARK_YAML: &str = include_str!("../themes/fleet-dark.yaml");
pub const GITHUB_DARK_YAML: &str = include_str!("../themes/github-dark.yaml");
```

Notes:

- Paths are relative to the file containing `include_str!`, i.e. `src/theme.rs` → `../themes/...`.
- This satisfies “embedded at compile time” and causes a compile error if a file is missing.

### 2.3 Make default theme YAML-backed with Rust fallback

Convert `default_dark()` to load from YAML first, and only fall back to hardcoded Rust values on failure:

```rust
impl Theme {
    /// Default dark theme (YAML-backed, with Rust fallback)
    pub fn default_dark() -> Self {
        match Theme::from_yaml(DEFAULT_DARK_YAML) {
            Ok(theme) => theme,
            Err(err) => {
                // Optional: log the failure if logging is available
                log::error!("Failed to load default-dark theme YAML: {err}, falling back to hardcoded theme");

                // Existing hardcoded theme as a safety net
                Theme {
                    name: "Default Dark".to_string(),
                    editor: EditorTheme {
                        background: Color::rgb(0x1E, 0x1E, 0x1E),
                        foreground: Color::rgb(0xD4, 0xD4, 0xD4),
                        current_line_background: Color::rgb(0x2A, 0x2A, 0x2A),
                        cursor_color: Color::rgb(0xFF, 0xFF, 0xFF),
                        selection_background: Color::rgb(0x26, 0x4F, 0x78),
                        secondary_cursor_color: Color::rgba(0xFF, 0xFF, 0xFF, 0x80),
                    },
                    gutter: GutterTheme {
                        background: Color::rgb(0x1E, 0x1E, 0x1E),
                        foreground: Color::rgb(0x85, 0x85, 0x85),
                        foreground_active: Color::rgb(0xC6, 0xC6, 0xC6),
                        border_color: Color::rgb(0x31, 0x34, 0x38),
                    },
                    status_bar: StatusBarTheme {
                        background: Color::rgb(0x00, 0x7A, 0xCC),
                        foreground: Color::rgb(0xFF, 0xFF, 0xFF),
                    },
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

- `Default for Theme` can stay as-is:

  ```rust
  impl Default for Theme {
      fn default() -> Self {
          Self::default_dark()
      }
  }
  ```

### 2.4 Add a simple built-in theme registry (for future UI)

In `theme.rs`, add a tiny registry:

```rust
pub struct BuiltinTheme {
    /// Stable identifier (e.g. for config: "default-dark", "fleet-dark")
    pub id: &'static str,
    /// Embedded YAML content
    pub yaml: &'static str,
}

pub const BUILTIN_THEMES: &[BuiltinTheme] = &[
    BuiltinTheme { id: "default-dark", yaml: DEFAULT_DARK_YAML },
    BuiltinTheme { id: "fleet-dark",   yaml: FLEET_DARK_YAML },
    BuiltinTheme { id: "github-dark",  yaml: GITHUB_DARK_YAML },
];

impl Theme {
    /// Load a built-in theme by id
    pub fn from_builtin(id: &str) -> Result<Self, String> {
        let entry = BUILTIN_THEMES
            .iter()
            .find(|t| t.id == id)
            .ok_or_else(|| format!("Unknown theme id: {id}"))?;
        Theme::from_yaml(entry.yaml)
    }
}
```

This gives you:

- A stable way to list themes in a future UI: `for t in BUILTIN_THEMES { ... }`.
- A simple config knob: store `"fleet-dark"` in some settings file and call `Theme::from_builtin("fleet-dark")`.

You can derive display names from the YAML at runtime (from `ThemeData.name`) instead of maintaining a second name field in the registry.

### 2.5 Migrate tests

Your existing tests will mostly keep working; you just change how the YAML consts are defined.

- `test_parse_fleet_dark` and `test_parse_github_dark` will now read from the files via `include_str!`, which is what you want:

  ```rust
  #[test]
  fn test_parse_fleet_dark() {
      let theme = Theme::from_yaml(FLEET_DARK_YAML).unwrap();
      assert_eq!(theme.name, "Fleet Dark");
      assert_eq!(theme.editor.background.r, 0x18);
  }

  #[test]
  fn test_parse_github_dark() {
      let theme = Theme::from_yaml(GITHUB_DARK_YAML).unwrap();
      assert_eq!(theme.name, "GitHub Dark");
      assert_eq!(theme.editor.background.r, 0x0D);
  }
  ```

- Add a quick test to ensure the default YAML loads:

  ```rust
  #[test]
  fn test_default_dark_yaml_parses() {
      let theme = Theme::from_yaml(DEFAULT_DARK_YAML).unwrap();
      assert_eq!(theme.name, "Default Dark");
  }
  ```

No other test changes needed.

### 2.6 Cargo.toml changes

For the simple `include_str!` embedding:

- **No Cargo.toml changes are required.**

You already have `serde` / `serde_yaml` set up. `include_str!` is a core language feature.

---

3) **Rationale and trade-offs**

- **Simplicity first**: `include_str!` is the least moving parts: no new crates, no `build.rs`, no tooling.
- **Edit themes without touching Rust code**: With this refactor, editing *existing* themes is just editing YAML under `themes//`. You only touch Rust when introducing brand new built-ins or changing the registry.
- **Safety**: Keeping the Rust `default_dark()` body as a fallback gives you robustness if someone breaks `default-dark.yaml`.
- **Future UI support**: A tiny `BUILTIN_THEMES` slice is enough for theme listing and selection; you can evolve the structure later if needed.

Why not auto-discover themes now?

- For a minimal editor with 2–5 built-ins, the manual registry is completely fine.
- Auto-discovery via `build.rs` adds complexity and a tricky extra step for contributors, for limited gain at this scale.

---

4) **Risks and guardrails**

- **Broken YAML causes runtime parse errors**
  - Guardrail: default theme uses hardcoded fallback; for other themes, propagate the `Result` and either fall back to default or show an error in the status bar.
- **Path mistakes in `include_str!`**
  - Guardrail: CI / `cargo test` will fail to compile if paths are wrong; that’s good. Keep the directory structure stable.
- **Theme registry drift** (YAML file added but not registered)
  - Guardrail: for now it’s acceptable. Document the workflow: “add YAML under `themes/` and register it in `BUILTIN_THEMES`.”

---

5) **When to consider the advanced path**

Consider a more complex design (e.g. `build.rs` auto-discovery) if:

- You start shipping many built-in themes (e.g. 10–20+).
- You expect non-Rust contributors to *regularly* add new bundled themes and want “drop in a file and rebuild” with zero Rust changes.
- You want to keep the registry entirely derived from the filesystem to avoid any manual duplication.

---

6) **Optional advanced path: build.rs-based auto-discovery**

If/when you want “add a theme just by adding a file”, you can:

1. **Add `build.rs`** at project root:

   - Scan the `themes/` directory with `std::fs::read_dir`.
   - For each `*.yaml`, write a generated `src/generated_themes.rs` file with something like:

     ```rust
     pub const BUILTIN_THEMES: &[(&str, &str)] = &[
         ("default-dark", include_str!(concat!(env!("CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR"), "/themes/default-dark.yaml"))),
         ("fleet-dark",   include_str!(concat!(env!("CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR"), "/themes/fleet-dark.yaml"))),
         ("github-dark",  include_str!(concat!(env!("CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR"), "/themes/github-dark.yaml"))),
         // auto-generated entries for any other .yaml files...
     ];
     ```

2. **In `src/theme.rs`**:

   ```rust
   mod generated_themes;
   use generated_themes::BUILTIN_THEMES;
   ```

3. **Runtime usage**:

   - Same as before: iterate `BUILTIN_THEMES`, parse YAML, etc.
   - New YAML files show up automatically after a rebuild.

4. **Cargo.toml**:

   - Still no mandatory dependencies. You only add a `[build-dependencies]` section if you want helper crates for scanning.

This advanced path gets you true “no Rust changes to add a theme” at the cost of a bit more infrastructure, and can be adopted later without breaking the current simple design.

---

That plan keeps your current code mostly intact, moves all theme contents into editable YAML, embeds them at compile time via `include_str!`, and gives you a small, ergonomic hook for future theme selection UI.
```

## Assistant

### Oracle Findings

**Current state**: Theme system already supports YAML parsing, but themes are defined as:
- `default_dark()` - hardcoded Rust values
- `FLEET_DARK_YAML` / `GITHUB_DARK_YAML` - inline const strings

#### Recommended Plan

**Folder Structure:**
```
├── themes/
│   ├── default-dark.yaml
│   ├── fleet-dark.yaml
│   └── github-dark.yaml
└── src/
    └── theme.rs
```

**Approach: `include_str!` embedding** (simplest, no build.rs needed)

```rust
pub const DEFAULT_DARK_YAML: &str = include_str!("../themes/default-dark.yaml");
pub const FLEET_DARK_YAML: &str = include_str!("../themes/fleet-dark.yaml");
pub const GITHUB_DARK_YAML: &str = include_str!("../themes/github-dark.yaml");
```

**Key changes:**
1. Create `themes/` folder with 3 YAML files
2. Replace inline YAML strings with `include_str!`
3. `default_dark()` loads from YAML, falls back to hardcoded Rust if parsing fails
4. Add `BUILTIN_THEMES` registry for future theme picker UI
5. No Cargo.toml changes needed

**Pros:**
- Edit themes without touching Rust
- Compile-time embedding (no runtime file loading)
- Compile error if theme file missing
- Simple, no build.rs complexity

**Effort:** ~30 min (S)

Want me to implement this plan?

Theme::from_yaml(entry.yaml)\n    }\n\n    /// Convert raw theme data to resolved theme\n    pub fn from_data(data: ThemeData) -> Result<Self, String> {\n        let default_selection_bg = Now I understand the layout. Let me summarize the current situation and what adding padding would affect:

**Current layout:**
- `LINE_NUMBER_GUTTER_CHARS = 6` (for "1234 " format with space)
- `text_start_x = char_width * 6` - where editor text begins
- `border_x = text_start_x - 1` - the 1px gutter border
- Text starts immediately at `text_start_x` (touching the border)

**Adding padding would affect:**
1. `text_start_x` calculation - needs to include padding
2. `visible_columns` calculation - slightly fewer visible columns
3. Selection rendering - uses `text_start_x`
4. Cursor rendering - uses `text_start_x`
5. Text drawing - uses `text_start_x`
6. Mouse click handling - uses `text_start_x` for position calculation

**Simplest approach:** Add a constant like `GUTTER_PADDING_PX` or express it in character units and add it to the gutter width calculation.

Here's my analysis:

---

### Current Layout

```
│1234 │Hello world...
      ↑
      text_start_x (border is 1px before this)
      Text touches the border
```

### With Padding

```
│1234 │  Hello world...
      ↑  ↑
      │  text_start_x (moved right by padding)
      border_x (unchanged)
```

### Affected Areas

| Component | File | What changes |
|-----------|------|--------------|
| `text_start_x` | main.rs:210, model/mod.rs:65,84,96 | Add padding to calculation |
| `visible_columns` | model/mod.rs | Slightly reduced |
| Selection highlight | main.rs:275-277, 312-313 | Uses `text_start_x` (auto-fixed) |
| Cursor drawing | main.rs:339, 431+ | Uses `text_start_x` (auto-fixed) |
| Text drawing | main.rs:397, 408 | Uses `text_start_x` (auto-fixed) |
| Mouse click → position | main.rs:541, 863 | Uses `text_start_x` (auto-fixed) |

### Recommended Approach

Add a new constant alongside `LINE_NUMBER_GUTTER_CHARS`:

```rust
pub const LINE_NUMBER_GUTTER_CHARS: usize = 6;
pub const EDITOR_LEFT_PADDING_CHARS: usize = 1; // ~1 char width of breathing room
```

Then update `text_start_x` calculation:
```rust
let text_start_x = (char_width * (LINE_NUMBER_GUTTER_CHARS + EDITOR_LEFT_PADDING_CHARS) as f32).round() as usize;
```

This keeps everything in character units (consistent with the rest of the layout) and all downstream calculations auto-adjust since they reference `text_start_x`.

occupies a separate horizontal region\n458: from the text area but shares the vertical scroll offset.\n459: \n460: This means:\n461: \n462: - When you scroll the text vertically, the gutter scrolls ## Assistant

Now I have a complete picture. Here's my analysis:

---

### Alignment with Reference Doc

The reference doc (Chapter 3, Appendix D) describes:

1. **Gutter as composite of lanes** with its own `gutterWidth`
2. **`gutterPadding: 8` pixels** on each side (Appendix D defaults)
3. **`textAreaBounds`** as a separate rectangle from `gutterBounds`
4. The border drawn at `gutterWidth - 0.5` (line 1888-1890)

#### Current Implementation vs Reference

| Aspect | Reference Doc | Current Code |
|--------|--------------|--------------|
| Gutter width | `gutterWidth` (includes padding) | `LINE_NUMBER_GUTTER_CHARS * char_width` |
| Text area start | `textAreaBounds.x` (after gutter) | `text_start_x` (same as gutter end) |
| Padding | `gutterPadding: 8px` each side | None (text touches border) |
| Border position | `gutterWidth - 0.5` | `text_start_x - 1` |

#### Recommended Alignment

The reference doc treats gutter and text area as **distinct regions with bounds**. To align:

```rust
// Layout constants
pub const LINE_NUMBER_GUTTER_CHARS: usize = 5;  // "1234 " (reduce from 6)
pub const GUTTER_RIGHT_PADDING_PX: f32 = 4.0;   // padding before border
pub const TEXT_LEFT_PADDING_PX: f32 = 8.0;      // padding after border (breathing room)

// Computed bounds
let gutter_width = (LINE_NUMBER_GUTTER_CHARS as f32 * char_width) + GUTTER_RIGHT_PADDING_PX;
let border_x = gutter_width.round() as usize;
let text_start_x = border_x + 1 + TEXT_LEFT_PADDING_PX as usize;
```

This gives:
```
│1234 ····│········Hello world...
         ↑        ↑
    border_x   text_start_x
    (gutter_right_padding)  (text_left_padding)
```

#### Simpler Alternative (Character-Based)

To stay consistent with current char-based approach:

```rust
pub const LINE_NUMBER_GUTTER_CHARS: usize = 6;  // "  42  " with padding
pub const TEXT_AREA_LEFT_MARGIN_CHARS: usize = 1;  // ~1 char breathing room
```

This is less precise but simpler and keeps all layout in character units.

---

**My recommendation**: Use the **pixel-based approach** for the padding (like the reference doc's `gutterPadding: 8`), since we're already mixing pixel and char units for the border. This gives more precise control and matches the reference doc's model.

## Assistant

Here's the issue:

**Line 266**: `line_len + 1` adds one extra character to visually show the newline is selected. This is intentional for lines that are NOT the last line of the selection.

However, the problem may be that this happens even when it shouldn't. Let me trace the logic:

- For middle lines of a multi-line selection: `end_col = line_len + 1` (shows newline)
- For the last line of selection: `end_col = sel_end.column` (correct)

This seems correct. But wait - the `+ 1` might be too much visually. Most editors show a small indicator, not a full character width.

**Options:**
1. Remove the `+ 1` entirely (newline not visually indicated)
2. Reduce it to a smaller visual indicator (e.g., `+ 0.5` or a few pixels)
3. Keep as-is (this is how VS Code works)

Let me check if VS Code does this too - it does show selection extending past line end. But perhaps the `+ 1` should only apply to lines that actually have a newline character?

## Assistant

The issue is clear now. The `+ 1` is unconditionally added to all non-final lines. But the last line of a document might not have a newline, yet if it's in the middle of a selection, it still gets `+ 1`.

**The fix**: Only add the newline indicator if the line actually ends with a newline.

Here's my finding:

---

### Issue Found

In [main.rs line 266](file://~/code/rust-editor/src/main.rs#L266):

```rust
line_len + 1 // Include newline character visually
```

This unconditionally adds a character width to show the "newline" is selected, but:
1. It adds a full character width (looks like extra whitespace)
2. It applies even to lines that don't have a newline (last line of file)

**Options:**

| Option | Description |
|--------|-------------|
| A | Remove entirely - no visual newline indicator |
| B | Only add if line has newline: check `get_line(doc_line).ends_with('\n')` |
| C | Use smaller indicator (e.g., half char width or fixed 4px) |

Most code editors (VS Code, Sublime) use option C - a small visual extension past the line end. Option B is also reasonable.

Which approach would you prefer?

## Assistant

I see - `move_cursor_word_right` moves through characters of the same "type" (word, whitespace, punctuation). So from position 0 ("hello world"):
- First call: moves from 0 to 5 (end of "hello")
- Second call: moves from 5 to 6 (skips the space, since space is Whitespace type)

This is expected behavior based on the word navigation style. My test expectations might be wrong. Let me trace through what happens with "hello world":
- Position 0-4: "hello" (WordChar)
- Position 5: " " (Whitespace)
- Position 6-10: "world" (WordChar)
- Position 11: "\n" (Whitespace)

So:
1. From pos 0, move right -> skip "hello" -> lands at 5
2. From pos 5, move right -> skip " " -> lands at 6
3. From pos 6, move right -> skip "world" -> lands at 11

The test expects to get to 11 after 2 moves, but the actual behavior takes 3 moves. Let me fix the tests to match the actual (correct) behavior:

## Assistant

All 124 tests pass.

### Summary

The issue was that `Shift+Option+Arrow` (word selection) was already mapped in the key handler, but the underlying `move_cursor_word_left/right` functions were calling `set_cursor_from_position()` which **cleared the selection**.

**Fix:**
1. Added `move_cursor_to_offset()` in `EditorState` - moves cursor without clearing selection
2. Added `move_cursor_to_position()` in `AppModel` - wrapper for the above
3. Updated `move_cursor_word_left/right` to use the new non-clearing method

**Tests added:**
- `test_word_selection_right_from_start` - select first word
- `test_word_selection_right_multiple` - select multiple words right
- `test_word_selection_left_from_end` - select backwards from end
- `test_word_selection_left_multiple` - select multiple words left
- `test_word_selection_extends_existing_selection` - extend existing selection

The feature now works correctly with `Shift+Option+Arrow` (macOS word selection with extend).

cursor should jump above the viewport,\n+        // and viewport should adjust to show cursor at top\n+        handle_key(&mut model, Key::Named(NamedKey::PageUp), false, false, false, false, false);\n+\n+        // PageUp moves visible_lines - 2 = 18 lines up\n+        // From line 10, that would be line 0 (clamped)\n+        assert_eq!(model.editor.cursor().line, 0, \"Cursor should be at line 0 after PageUp\");\n+\n+        // Viewport should adjust to show cursor\n+        // With cursor at line 0, viewport.top_line should be 0\n+        assert_eq!(\n+            model.editor.viewport.top_line, 0,\n+            \"Viewport should scroll to top to show cursor\"\n+        );\n+\n+        // Cursor should be visible\n+        assert!(\n+            model.editor.cursor().line >= model.editor.viewport.top_line,\n+            \"Cursor should be visible (>= viewport top)\"\n+        );\n+    }\n+\n+    #[test]\n+    fn test_pageup_from_middle_adjusts_viewport() {\n+        // Create a 100-line document\n+        let text = (0..100).map(|i| format!(\"line {}\\n\", i)).collect::<String>();\n+        let mut model = test_model_with_selection(&text, 0, 0, 0, 0);\n+        model.editor.viewport.visible_lines = 20;\n+        model.editor.scroll_padding = 1;\n+\n+        // Position cursor at line 50 with viewport showing lines 40-60\n+        model.editor.cursor_mut().line = 50;\n+        model.editor.viewport.top_line = 40;\n+\n+        // Press PageUp - 18)\n+        assert_eq!(model.editor.cursor().line, 32, \"Cursor should be at line 32\");\n+\n+        // Line 32 was above the viewport (which was at 40-60)\n+        // Viewport should have adjusted to show the cursor\n+        // Cursor should be visible and near the top of viewport\n+        assert!(\n+            model.editor.viewport.top_line <= model.editor.cursor().line,\n+            \"Cursor (line {}) should be >= viewport top (line {})\",\n+            model.editor.cursor().line,\n+            model.editor.viewport.top_line\n+        );\n+\n+        // Cursor should be within visible range\n+        let viewport_end = model.editor.viewport.top_line + model.editor.viewport.visible_lines;\n+        assert!(\n+            model.editor.cursor().line < viewport_end,\n+            \"Cursor should be visible within viewport\"\n+        );\n+    }\n+}\n```",

## Assistant

All 127 tests pass.

### Summary

Added 3 new test cases for large document viewport focus:

1. **`test_select_all_then_right_arrow_scrolls_to_end`** - Creates a 500-line document, selects all, presses right arrow. Verifies cursor moves to end AND viewport scrolls to show the cursor.

2. **`test_pageup_scrolls_cursor_to_viewport_top`** - Tests that PageUp from half-viewport height properly scrolls viewport to show cursor.

3. **`test_pageup_from_middle_adjusts_viewport`** - Tests that PageUp from line 50 (with viewport at 40-60) properly adjusts viewport when cursor jumps above visible area.

**Bug fixed:** The tests revealed that arrow keys with selection (`ArrowLeft`, `ArrowRight`) were NOT calling `ensure_cursor_visible()` after clearing selection and repositioning the cursor. This caused the viewport to stay in place while the cursor could jump far off-screen. Fixed by adding `model.ensure_cursor_visible()` to both handlers.
